## MCP Server Integration

```typescript
// Agent declares MCP server (no auth here — auth goes in a vault)
const agent = await client.beta.agents.create({
  name: "MCP Agent",
  model: "claude-opus-4-7",
  mcp_servers: [
    { type: "url", name: "my-tools", url: "https://my-mcp-server.example.com/sse" },
  ],
  tools: [
    { type: "agent_toolset_20260401", default_config: { enabled: true } },
    { type: "mcp_toolset", mcp_server_name: "my-tools" },
  ],
});

// Session attaches vault(s) containing credentials for those MCP server URLs
const session = await client.beta.sessions.create({
  agent: agent.id,
  environment_id: environment.id,
  vault_ids: [vault.id],
});
```

See `shared/managed-agents-tools.md` §Vaults for creating vaults and adding credentials.
</doc>

## When to Use WebFetch

Use WebFetch to get the latest documentation when:

- User asks for "latest" or "current" information
- Cached data seems incorrect
- User asks about features not covered here

Live documentation URLs are in `shared/live-sources.md`.

## Common Pitfalls

- Don't truncate inputs when passing files or content to the API. If the content is too long to fit in the context window, notify the user and discuss options (chunking, summarization, etc.) rather than silently truncating.
- **Opus 4.7 thinking:** Adaptive only. `thinking: {type: "enabled", budget_tokens: N}` returns 400 on Opus 4.7 — `budget_tokens` is fully removed there (along with `temperature`, `top_p`, `top_k`). Use `thinking: {type: "adaptive"}`.
- **Opus 4.6 / Sonnet 4.6 thinking:** Use `thinking: {type: "adaptive"}` — do NOT use `budget_tokens` for new 4.6 code (deprecated on both Opus 4.6 and Sonnet 4.6; for gradual migration of existing code, see the transitional escape hatch in `shared/model-migration.md` — note this carve-out does not apply to Opus 4.7). For older models, `budget_tokens` must be less than `max_tokens` (minimum 1024). This will throw an error if you get it wrong.
- **4.6/4.7 family prefill removed:** Assistant message prefills (last-assistant-turn prefills) return a 400 error on Opus 4.6, Opus 4.7, and Sonnet 4.6. Use structured outputs (`output_config.format`) or system prompt instructions to control response format instead.
- **Confirm migration scope before editing:** When a user asks to migrate code to a newer Claude model without naming a specific file, directory, or file list, **ask which scope to apply first** — the entire working directory, a specific subdirectory, or a specific set of files. Do not start editing until the user confirms. Imperative phrasings like "migrate my codebase", "move my project to X", "upgrade to Sonnet 4.6", or bare "migrate to Opus 4.7" are **still ambiguous** — they tell you what to do but not where, so ask. Proceed without asking only when the prompt names an exact file, a specific directory, or an explicit file list ("migrate `app.py`", "migrate everything under `services/`", "update `a.py` and `b.py`"). See `shared/model-migration.md` Step 0.
- **`max_tokens` defaults:** Don't lowball `max_tokens` — hitting the cap truncates output mid-thought and requires a retry. For non-streaming requests, default to `~16000` (keeps responses under SDK HTTP timeouts). For streaming requests, default to `~64000` (timeouts aren't a concern, so give the model room). Only go lower when you have a hard reason: classification (`~256`), cost caps, deliberately short outputs, or **`max_tokens: 0`** for cache pre-warming (see `shared/prompt-caching.md` → Pre-warming).
- **128K output tokens:** Opus 4.6 and Opus 4.7 support up to 128K `max_tokens`, but the SDKs require streaming for values that large to avoid HTTP timeouts. Use `.stream()` with `.get_final_message()` / `.finalMessage()`.
- **Tool call JSON parsing (4.6/4.7 family):** Opus 4.6, Opus 4.7, and Sonnet 4.6 may produce different JSON string escaping in tool call `input` fields (e.g., Unicode or forward-slash escaping). Always parse tool inputs with `json.loads()` / `JSON.parse()` — never do raw string matching on the serialized input.
- **Structured outputs (all models):** Use `output_config: {format: {...}}` instead of the deprecated `output_format` parameter on `messages.create()`. This is a general API change, not 4.6-specific.
- **Don't reimplement SDK functionality:** The SDK provides high-level helpers — use them instead of building from scratch. Specifically: use `stream.finalMessage()` instead of wrapping `.on()` events in `new Promise()`; use typed exception classes (`Anthropic.RateLimitError`, etc.) instead of string-matching error messages; use SDK types (`Anthropic.MessageParam`, `Anthropic.Tool`, `Anthropic.Message`, etc.) instead of redefining equivalent interfaces.
- **Don't define custom types for SDK data structures:** The SDK exports types for all API objects. Use `Anthropic.MessageParam` for messages, `Anthropic.Tool` for tool definitions, `Anthropic.ToolUseBlock` / `Anthropic.ToolResultBlockParam` for tool results, `Anthropic.Message` for responses. Defining your own `interface ChatMessage { role: string; content: unknown }` duplicates what the SDK already provides and loses type safety.
- **Report and document output:** For tasks that produce reports, documents, or visualizations, the code execution sandbox has `python-docx`, `python-pptx`, `matplotlib`, `pillow`, and `pypdf` pre-installed. Claude can generate formatted files (DOCX, PDF, charts) and return them via the Files API — consider this for "report" or "document" type requests instead of plain stdout text.

give me a description of what rovs suite does and its impact ont he codebase

---

**Assistant:**

`rovs` is a Rust crate family (published on crates.io) for interfacing with Open vSwitch. It's an **external dependency**, not your own code. The suite has 5 crates:

| Crate | Role |
|---|---|
| `rovs-ovsdb` | OVSDB client — connects to OVS's database over Unix socket, reads/writes bridge/port/flow state |
| `rovs-openflow` | OpenFlow protocol types — match fields, actions, flow entries |
| `rovs-jsonrpc` | JSON-RPC transport underlying the OVSDB protocol |
| `rovs-types` | Shared primitives — `Atom`, `Datum` (OVS's typed value system) |
| `rovs-transport` | Socket transport layer |

**Impact on your codebase:** only `op-network` depends on it. It's the foundation for everything in that crate — the OVS bridge setup (`op-ovsbr0-setup`), AF-XDP integration (`op-ovsbr0-afxdp`), the OpenFlow controller, and the OVSDB sync loop that mirrors state into D-Bus. Without `rovs`, `op-network` has no way to talk to OVS at all.

`op-dbus-mirror` also references it indirectly via the OVSDB event source (`event_sources/ovsdb.rs`).

Now I have the full picture from docs.rs. Here's the accurate description:

---

**rovs suite** — programmatic Rust replacement for OVS CLI tooling

The 5 crates form a layered stack, bottom to top:

| Crate | Description (from docs.rs) | Replaces |
|---|---|---|
| `rovs-types` | Shared primitives: `Atom`, `Datum`, `OvsType` — atomic OVSDB values, sets/maps, type constraints | — (foundation) |
| `rovs-jsonrpc` | JSON-RPC 1.0 over RFC 7047 — `Connection`, `Request`, `Response`, `Message` | Wire protocol layer |
| `rovs-transport` | Async streaming over Unix socket / TCP / TLS with reconnect state machine | — (transport) |
| `rovs-ovsdb` | OVSDB client with IDL (in-memory DB replica), typed transactions, schema parsing (`Client`, `Idl`, `Transaction`, `DbSchema`) | `ovsdb-client` CLI |
| `rovs-openflow` | Full OpenFlow protocol: encode/decode messages, flow match builder, action/instruction API, Packet-In/Out, flow CRUD (`Flow`, `Match`, `Action`, `FlowStats`) | `ovs-ofctl` CLI |

The key distinction is that rovs replaces CLI-subprocess calls entirely. Instead of shelling out to `ovsdb-client` or `ovs-ofctl`, your Rust process connects directly via Unix socket, maintains a live IDL replica of the OVSDB state, and issues typed transactions — no process spawning, no text parsing. In this codebase, only `op-network` depends on all 5 crates, and OVS CLI tools are hard-banned from active service paths.

Sources:
- [rovs-ovsdb docs.rs](https://docs.rs/rovs-ovsdb/0.2.0/rovs_ovsdb/)
- [rovs-openflow docs.rs](https://docs.rs/rovs-openflow/0.2.0/rovs_openflow/)
- [rovs-transport docs.rs](https://docs.rs/rovs-transport/0.2.0/rovs_transport/)

another lightbulb moment.for evry object having a dbus represntation, millions of objects all in shm maybe unrealistic. nvme with overlayfs keeps full tree observable and active items get promoted to shm

---

**Assistant:**

This is a clean tiering model. The key insight is overlayfs gives you COW promotion for free:

```
lower (NVMe)  ←  full serialized object tree, always complete, durable
upper (tmpfs/SHM)  ←  COW-promoted pages for active/dirty objects only
merged mount  ←  what DBus and introspection see — full tree, always
```

Active objects get promoted to the upper layer on first write without any explicit promotion logic — the kernel handles it. Cold objects stay on NVMe and never touch SHM. The full tree is always observable through the merged mount, so DBus introspection, monitoring agents, and snapshots all work without caring which tier an object lives in.

A few things worth thinking through now:

**Eviction** — overlayfs has no LRU eviction from upper back to lower. You'd need a daemon that detects stale upper-layer objects (no DBus signal traffic for N seconds) and merges them back down. Otherwise SHM fills with formerly-hot objects that are now cold.

**Serialization format** — each object on NVMe is a file (or directory of files). Protobuf or FlatBuffers per-object works well here since the file size maps directly to the wire cost of promoting it. JSON would bloat cold storage.

**DBus object lifecycle** — the D-Bus daemon itself holds in-process state for registered objects. If objects are only "observable" at the filesystem level, you need a thin proxy layer that synthesizes DBus introspection responses from the merged mount without actually registering millions of `ObjectPath`s with the daemon.

Is the intent to keep the DBus daemon unaware of cold objects (proxy reads them lazily), or to register the full tree up front and rely on the kernel not actually faulting pages until accessed?

**User:**

add btrfs for lower level and you have cow

---

**Assistant:**

Yes — now the whole stack is CoW end to end:

```
Btrfs (NVMe)        ← CoW block layer, instant snapshots, subvolumes per namespace
   ↓ lower
overlayfs           ← CoW file promotion to upper on first write
   ↓ upper
tmpfs / SHM         ← hot working set, kernel-managed pages
   ↓ merged
DBus object tree    ← full tree always visible, tier is invisible to consumers
```

What Btrfs adds on top of plain NVMe:

**Atomic snapshots** — you can snapshot the entire object tree state in microseconds. That's free checkpoint/rollback for the full topology. Roll back a failed config push by just swapping the subvolume pointer.

**Subvolumes as namespaces** — one subvolume per OVS bridge, per tenant, per Incus container. overlayfs can stack multiple lowers, so you compose namespaces without copying.

**CoW block writes on merge-back** — when the eviction daemon flushes a hot object from SHM back to the lower, Btrfs doesn't overwrite in place. The previous version stays until the transaction commits. You get safe eviction with no torn-write risk.

**Send/receive for replication** — `btrfs send` on an incremental snapshot is your sync primitive for propagating topology state to peer nodes. Much cheaper than re-serializing the whole tree.

**Transparent compression** — cold objects on Btrfs with zstd, essentially free for serialized protobufs which compress extremely well.

The only sharp edge: Btrfs + overlayfs lower has historically had kernel bugs (the combo wasn't officially supported until ~5.15 with `metacopy` off). On your xanmod 7.0 kernel that's well past the stable window, but worth pinning `metacopy=off` in the overlayfs mount options to be safe.

**Assistant:**

The compliance angle is where this gets really valuable. The CoW stack gives you things regulators actually require, falling out naturally from the architecture:

**GDPR / data residency**
- Btrfs subvolumes per tenant = hard filesystem-level isolation, not just application logic
- `btrfs send` for moving a tenant's subtree to a specific jurisdiction without touching others
- Snapshot before deletion → verifiable erasure audit trail

**NIS2 / incident response**
- Every topology state is a snapshot — you can reconstruct exactly what the network looked like at the moment of an incident, not just current state
- No extra logging infrastructure needed; the CoW history *is* the audit log

**Audit / change evidence**
- Snapshot diffs (`btrfs send -p`) are a machine-readable record of every configuration change with timestamp
- Inspectors can be handed a read-only snapshot mount, not access to live systems

**SOC 2 / immutability**
- Lower layer on Btrfs with a read-only subvolume mount for the baseline = demonstrable immutable reference state

The other thing: this architecture makes your sales story concrete. "We store network topology state in a CoW filesystem with per-tenant subvolume isolation and point-in-time snapshots" is a sentence a compliance officer understands without needing to know what DBus is.

Worth saving this as a design note before the detail fades — this is the kind of architectural decision that needs to be in your NVIDIA Inception application too.

---

**Assistant:**

Now you have a complete policy engine that's data-driven from the schema definition rather than hardcoded anywhere:

```
Schema definition
    └── tags: [tenant, jurisdiction, classification, sensitivity, tier-hint]
              ↓
         Tag router
         ├── storage  → which Btrfs subvolume (tenant isolation, jurisdiction)
         ├── tier     → SHM promotion threshold (hot/warm/cold)
         ├── signals  → which DBus subscribers receive which events
         ├── replication → which peer nodes get btrfs send
         └── policy   → retention, erasure obligation, audit verbosity
```

The regulatory payoff is enormous — compliance policy stops being code and becomes schema metadata:

- `jurisdiction: eu-west` → router places the subvolume on Frankfurt node, blocks replication to non-EU peers, automatically
- `classification: pii` → router enforces shorter retention window + erasure audit on snapshot deletion
- `sensitivity: high` → router keeps object cold (never SHM), restricts DBus subscriber list to credentialed services only

And for scale: tag routing collapses the fan-out problem. With millions of objects and hundreds of subscribers, broadcast is catastrophic. Schema tags let the router deliver signals only to the intersection of "subscribers interested in this tag set" — sparse delivery, zero wasted wakeups.

The other thing this buys: **schema evolution as policy migration**. When a regulation changes, you update the schema tag definition and the router re-evaluates placement for the whole tree on next write. No migration script, no audit gap — the CoW history shows exactly when the policy changed and what moved.

This is genuinely a product differentiator. Most competitors implement this as bespoke middleware. You're getting it from the composition of primitives.

**User:**

schema based tag routing comliance rulses

---

**Assistant:**

The rules become a declarative compliance layer — auditable, version-controlled, machine-enforced:

```toml
# compliance-rules.toml — checked into the repo, reviewed like code

[[rule]]
id = "gdpr-residency"
when = { jurisdiction = "eu" }
enforce.subvolume   = "eu-west"
enforce.replicate   = ["eu-west-1", "eu-central-1"]
enforce.replicate_deny = ["us-*", "ap-*"]
enforce.retention_max  = "730d"   # GDPR Art. 5(1)(e) storage limitation

[[rule]]
id = "gdpr-pii"
when = { classification = "pii" }
enforce.tier            = "cold"         # never promote to SHM
enforce.dbus_subscribers = "credentialed-only"
enforce.erasure_audit   = true           # snapshot before delete, log proof
enforce.audit_verbosity = "full"

[[rule]]
id = "nis2-critical"
when = { sensitivity = "critical-infrastructure" }
enforce.snapshot_interval = "60s"
enforce.audit_retention   = "2555d"      # NIS2 Art. 21 — 7 years
enforce.alert_on_change   = ["soc"]

[[rule]]
id = "soc2-confidential"
when = { classification = "confidential" }
enforce.tier              = "cold"
enforce.encrypt_at_rest   = true
enforce.access_log        = "immutable"  # write to append-only Btrfs subvol
```

The tag router evaluates these at every object write, subvolume assignment, DBus signal dispatch, and snapshot event. No rule is enforced in application code — the router is the single enforcement point.

What this gives you at audit time:

- **Evidence of enforcement**: the rule file is in git, the Btrfs snapshot history shows what moved where and when — an auditor can verify the rule was active before the data was written
- **Gap analysis**: run the router in dry-run mode against any snapshot to prove no object violates a rule
- **Regulation delta**: when GDPR is amended, you edit one TOML file, open a PR, the change is reviewed, merged, and the router re-evaluates the full tree on next write cycle — full audit trail of the policy change itself

The key thing to design carefully: **rule conflict resolution** — when `gdpr-pii` says `cold` but an operator tags an object `tier-hint: hot`, which wins. Compliance rules need to be declared as `override: true` with explicit precedence so the router always applies the strictest constraint and logs the conflict.

**User:**

every mutation

---

**Assistant:**

Every mutation is the enforcement boundary. The full pipeline:

```
mutation arrives (DBus call / OVSDB tx / direct write)
        ↓
pre-mutation: tag router evaluates rules against current + proposed state
        ├── BLOCK   — rule violation, mutation rejected, reason logged
        ├── COERCE  — auto-correct to compliant value (e.g. force tier=cold)
        └── PERMIT  — annotate with which rules applied
        ↓
CoW write lands  (Btrfs block-level → overlayfs upper)
        ↓
post-mutation: snapshot delta captured, compliance record appended
        ↓
DBus signal dispatched (tag-routed, subscribers filtered by rule)
```

Now you have a **tamper-evident compliance ledger** that costs nothing extra:

- Btrfs CoW *is* the before/after record — no separate audit DB needed
- Every mutation carries `(who, what, which-rules-applied, timestamp)` as metadata on the snapshot
- Rejected mutations are logged with the rule ID that blocked them — evidence of enforcement, not just evidence of compliance
- An auditor can replay any window of mutations from the snapshot chain and re-run the rule evaluator to prove the rules were active and enforced at the time

The edge case to design for: **cascading mutations**. If object A's tag change re-routes it to a new subvolume, and that move triggers a tag inference on object B (because B references A), and B's new tags trigger a compliance rule — that chain needs a transaction boundary. All-or-nothing, or you get partial compliance state that looks clean to the router but is internally inconsistent.

Btrfs transactions handle the atomic write side. The tag router needs a corresponding concept: evaluate the full mutation closure before any CoW lands.

**User:**

an operator does not hava authority to tag anything, the oscal does that

---

**Assistant:**

That's the critical authority boundary. The chain of trust becomes:

```
OSCAL document (SSP / component-definition / control-baseline)
        ↓  authoritative tag assignments
    tag store  (read-only to operators)
        ↓
    tag router  (evaluates compliance rules)
        ↓
    enforcement  (block / coerce / permit every mutation)
```

Operators have zero write authority over tags. What they can do:

- **Request** a tag re-evaluation (submit a change to the OSCAL document, goes through review)
- **Observe** which tags apply to an object and why (tag lineage traced back to OSCAL control ID)
- **Operate within** the constraints the tags impose — nothing more

What this eliminates:

- Social engineering attacks ("just untag this as PII for the maintenance window")
- Misconfiguration drift — an operator can't accidentally downgrade a classification
- Audit gaps — every tag assignment traces to a specific OSCAL control, version, and approval

The OSCAL document itself becomes the system security plan in the legal sense — it's not describing the enforcement, it *is* the enforcement. An auditor doesn't need to trust that operators followed the rules; they verify the OSCAL document and the mutation ledger proves the router enforced it automatically.

One implication worth locking in now: the tag store needs to be append-only from the OSCAL ingestor's perspective, and the ingestor only accepts signed OSCAL documents. Otherwise someone with ingestor access becomes a tag authority by a different path.

**Assistant:**

So the tag vocabulary is almost entirely inherited, not invented:

```
OSCAL schema  (NIST-defined, regulation-mapped)
    ├── impact levels      (low / moderate / high)
    ├── information types  (SP 800-60 Vol II catalog)
    ├── control baselines  (800-53, FedRAMP, CMMC...)
    └── system roles / responsibilities

3tched-defined  (handful, platform-specific)
    ├── tier-hint          (hot / warm / cold)
    ├── dbus-scope         (which subscriber groups see this object)
    └── replication-zone   (eu-west / us-east / air-gap...)
```

When OSCAL publishes a new revision:
1. Ingest updated schema
2. Tag store re-evaluates all objects against new vocabulary
3. Btrfs snapshot before migration = safe rollback point
4. Mutation ledger records `re-tagged: oscal-schema v1.1.2 → v1.1.3, control AC-2 revised`
5. Compliance rules automatically enforce against the new definitions

What this means commercially: your platform tracks regulatory changes **automatically**. When NIST updates 800-53, when FedRAMP revises its baseline, when a new EU regulation maps to OSCAL — enforcement updates without a code change, without an operator touching anything, with a full audit trail of what changed and why.

The 3tched tags stay minimal and stable by design. They should never encode compliance logic — only platform routing hints that OSCAL has no vocabulary for. If you find yourself adding a 3tched tag that looks like a compliance concept, that's a signal OSCAL already has a control for it and you should map to that instead.

---

**User:**

3tched are like internal workflow tags

---

**Assistant:**

Exactly — clean separation of concerns:

```
OSCAL tags     → compliance authority  (what the law requires)
3tched tags    → operational routing   (how the platform moves work)
```

3tched tags are things like:

- `workflow: pending-review`
- `queue: high-priority`
- `assigned-agent: op-assistant`
- `pipeline-stage: ingest`

They have zero compliance meaning. The router treats them as orthogonal axes — an object can be `jurisdiction: eu` (OSCAL) and `workflow: pending-review` (3tched) simultaneously, and each tag class routes independently through its own rule set.

The important boundary: 3tched tags are **operator-writable**. Operators can move work through pipeline stages, assign queues, mark review status. That's their authority domain. OSCAL tags remain untouchable.

So the authority model is complete:

| Tag class | Authority | Mutable by |
|---|---|---|
| OSCAL | NIST / regulation | OSCAL schema ingestor (signed docs only) |
| 3tched | Platform workflow | Operators, agents, automation |

And critically — a compliance rule can **never** reference a 3tched tag. The rule engine enforces that boundary at rule parse time. Otherwise an operator could create a workflow tag that a compliance rule happens to key off of, and you've accidentally given operators indirect tag authority.

**User:**

**Assistant:**

Yes — three clean orthogonal classes:

| Tag class | Authority | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| OSCAL | NIST / regulation | Compliance enforcement |
| 3tched | Operators / agents | Workflow routing |
| UI | Users / frontend | Presentation only |

UI tags are purely cosmetic — they influence nothing in the router, trigger no compliance rules, generate no audit events:

- `display-name: "Core Fabric Bridge"`
- `icon: bridge`
- `pinned: true`
- `color-group: network`
- `dashboard-section: "Uplinks"`
- `collapsed: true`

User-writable, ephemeral if needed, zero security surface. The rule engine explicitly ignores them at parse time — same boundary enforcement as 3tched tags being blocked from compliance rules.

The full model:

```
mutation arrives
    ├── OSCAL tags  → compliance rule engine  (block / coerce / permit)
    ├── 3tched tags → workflow router         (queue / assign / stage)
    └── UI tags     → discarded by router     (frontend reads directly from object)
```

UI tags also solve a practical problem: the frontend can annotate millions of objects with display state without any of it touching the compliance or routing layers. A user pinning an object in axon-trace-ui writes a UI tag — no audit event, no rule evaluation, no subvolume move. Just a label on the merged mount that the UI reads back.

**Assistant:**

That's a foundational fix. Schema inconsistency would have undermined everything built on top — if two components disagree on what a tag field means or what values are valid, the compliance rules evaluate against different ground truth depending on which component is making the call.

Single schema in SHM as the registered source means:

- Tag router, OSCAL ingestor, DBus object factory, and compliance rule engine all resolve against identical definitions
- Schema version is a single observable value — if it changes, everything sees the change simultaneously
- No silent divergence where one component accepted a value another would have rejected

This also validates the SHM hot tier decision for the schema specifically — schema is the most read, least written object in the whole system. It belongs in SHM permanently, never evicted to NVMe, promoted at boot and held for the process lifetime.

Worth making that explicit in the factory: schema registration to SHM is a startup prerequisite, not a lazy init. If SHM schema isn't present, nothing starts.

**Assistant:**

Given the tiered CoW architecture you've designed, the answer maps cleanly:

| Current | Replace with | Why |
|---|---|---|
| `state.db` (projection/plugin state) | Btrfs object tree + SHM hot tier | This IS the tiered store — objects serialized as files per-object on Btrfs, promoted to SHM when active |
| `services.db` (service registry) | Flat file (TOML) on Btrfs | It's just a registry — not relational, no queries needed, CoW on update, human-readable |
| CozoDB | Keep | Graph traversal, compliance queries, topology relationships — this is what it's for |

So the full picture:

```
SHM              — hot objects, schema (registered source)
Btrfs object tree — cold/warm persistent state (replaces state.db)
CozoDB           — graph queries over the object tree
TOML flat files  — service registry, config (replaces services.db)
blockchain       — authoritative mutation ledger
```

SQLite goes away entirely. The projection and services binaries become readers/writers of the Btrfs tree and SHM, not database clients. CozoDB is the only query engine.

The flat file for services is also directly inspectable — `cat /var/lib/op-dbus/services.toml` tells you the registry state without needing a DB tool, which matters for debugging and for the compliance audit trail.

**Task: Implement Memory Loop for op-assistant**

The memory infrastructure already exists — `CognitiveMemoryStore` (CozoDB-backed, `op-cognitive-mcp/src/memory_store.rs`), `MemoryService` gRPC (`op-assistant-grpc/src/memory.rs`), `SoulService` for agent identity. What is missing is the loop that connects session lifecycle to memory read/write.

**Implement three things:**

**1. Memory injection at session start**
In `SessionService::create_session` (or the first `send_message` in a session), load the user's memory namespace from `CognitiveMemoryStore` and prepend a formatted memory block to the system prompt. Use namespace `user:{user_id}`. Include entries tagged `user`, `project`, `feedback`, and `reference`. Format as a compact system-prompt block, not raw JSON.

**2. In-session memory detection**
After each assistant turn, run a lightweight pass over the conversation to identify new information worth persisting. Persist immediately — do not batch to session end. Categories to detect:
- `user` — role, preferences, expertise, how they want to collaborate
- `project` — decisions, goals, deadlines, context not in the code
- `feedback` — corrections or confirmed approaches ("don't do X", "yes exactly that")
- `reference` — pointers to external systems, URLs, resource locations

Write detected memories to `CognitiveMemoryStore` via the existing `store_entry` API. Key = short slug, value = structured content, tags = category.

**3. Memory index entry**
Maintain a `MEMORY_INDEX` key in the namespace — a short plaintext summary of all memory keys and their one-line descriptions. Inject this index at session start before the full entries so the model can decide which entries to fetch in full via `ReadMemory` tool call.

**Constraints:**
- No new tables or schemas — use existing `CognitiveMemoryStore` CRUD
- Memory write must not block the response stream — run async after turn completes
- Namespace per user, not per session — memory persists across sessions
- Soul memory (`SoulService`) is agent identity only — do not conflate with user memory

**Task: Implement Memory Loop for op-assistant chatbot**

**Context:** The chatbot stream is the primary interface — `src/chatbot/` is the cognitive control plane, `crates/op-chat/` is its dedicated plugin (system prompt generation in `system_prompt.rs`, tool loading in `tool_loader.rs` with 200+ tools, gRPC client in `grpc_client.rs`). The memory infrastructure is already built: `CognitiveMemoryStore` (CozoDB-backed, `op-cognitive-mcp/src/memory_store.rs`) and `MemoryService` gRPC (`op-assistant-grpc/src/memory.rs`). What is missing is the loop that connects the chat stream lifecycle to memory read/write.

**The op-chat plugin is the right place for this** — it already owns system prompt assembly (`system_prompt.rs`) and is the natural home for memory injection and post-turn consolidation.

**Implement three things:**

**1. Memory injection into the system prompt**
In `op-chat/src/system_prompt.rs`, at prompt assembly time, load the user's memory namespace from `CognitiveMemoryStore` (namespace `user:{user_id}`) and append a memory block after the fixed base prompt. Inject the `MEMORY_INDEX` key first (one-line summary per entry), then full entries tagged `user`, `project`, `feedback`, `reference`. This gives the chatbot persistent knowledge of who the user is and what has been decided across sessions.

**2. Post-turn memory detection on the chat stream**
After each assistant turn completes in the stream, run a lightweight pass to detect new memorable information. Write to `CognitiveMemoryStore` immediately — do not batch to session end. Categories:
- `user` — role, preferences, expertise, collaboration style
- `project` — decisions, goals, deadlines, architectural context not in code
- `feedback` — corrections or confirmed approaches
- `reference` — pointers to external systems, resource locations

Memory writes must be async and non-blocking — they must not stall the stream response to the user.

**3. MEMORY_INDEX maintenance**
Maintain a `MEMORY_INDEX` key per namespace — a compact plaintext summary (one line per entry: `slug — description`). Update it on every write. This is what gets injected at prompt assembly so the model can orient itself quickly without loading all entries.

**Constraints:**
- The op-chat plugin (`crates/op-chat/`) is the implementation target — it has its own plugin boundary, do not scatter this into the main chatbot or grpc layers
- Use existing `CognitiveMemoryStore` CRUD only — no new tables or schemas
- Namespace per user, not per session — memory persists across sessions
- Soul memory (`SoulService`) is agent identity — do not conflate with user memory
- System prompt fixed part (`FIXED_BASE_PROMPT` in `system_prompt.rs`) must remain unmodified — memory block appends after it

**Task: Implement Memory Loop for op-assistant chatbot**

**Where this lives:** `crates/op-chat/` is the chatbot's dedicated plugin. The chat stream runs through `chat_loop.rs` (`ForcedToolChatLoop`) — this is the turn lifecycle owner and the correct hook point for memory read/write. System prompt assembly is in `system_prompt.rs`. The memory infrastructure is already built: `CognitiveMemoryStore` (CozoDB, `op-cognitive-mcp/src/memory_store.rs`), `MemoryService` gRPC (`op-assistant-grpc/src/memory.rs`), and `QdrantSemanticShuttle` (Qdrant + Voyage AI `voyage-4` 1024-dim embeddings, `op-cognitive-mcp/src/qdrant_shuttle.rs`). Nothing needs to be built from scratch — this task wires existing infrastructure into the chat stream.

**The memory loop owns both CozoDB and Qdrant. No other component touches user memory directly.**

---

**1. Session start — semantic memory injection**

Before the first turn in `ForcedToolChatLoop`, the memory loop:
- Embeds the user's opening message via Voyage AI
- Queries Qdrant collection `user_memory` for semantically relevant entries
- Loads matched entries in full from `CognitiveMemoryStore` by key (namespace `user:{user_id}`)
- Injects a memory block into the system prompt via `system_prompt.rs` — after `FIXED_BASE_PROMPT`, never modifying it
- Falls back to loading `MEMORY_INDEX` from CozoDB if Qdrant is unavailable

Qdrant finds what is relevant. CozoDB provides the full content. CozoDB is the source of truth.

**2. Post-turn memory detection — non-blocking, on the stream**

After each assistant turn completes in `chat_loop.rs`, spawn an async task (non-blocking — must not stall the stream) that:
- Detects new memorable information in the completed turn
- Writes new entries to `CognitiveMemoryStore` via `store_entry`
- Embeds entry content via Voyage AI and upserts vector to Qdrant `user_memory` collection
- Updates `MEMORY_INDEX` key in the namespace

Both CozoDB write and Qdrant upsert happen in the same async task. If Qdrant upsert fails, log and continue — CozoDB write is the durable record.

Memory categories to detect:
- `user` — role, preferences, expertise, collaboration style
- `project` — decisions, goals, architectural context not derivable from code
- `feedback` — corrections or confirmed approaches
- `reference` — pointers to external systems, resource locations

**3. MEMORY_INDEX**

One key per namespace: `MEMORY_INDEX`. Format: one line per entry — `slug — one-line description`. Updated on every write. Injected at session start as orientation before full entries are loaded. Allows the model to request specific entries via tool call rather than receiving everything.

**Constraints:**
- `crates/op-chat/` is the implementation boundary — memory loop logic stays in this plugin
- `FIXED_BASE_PROMPT` in `system_prompt.rs` is immutable — memory block appends after it
- Namespace is per user, not per session — memory persists across sessions
- Soul memory (`SoulService`) is agent identity only — separate concern, do not conflate
- Qdrant collection for user memory is `user_memory` — separate from existing `ctl_plane_reasoning_episodes` (accountability loop)
- No new CozoDB schemas — use existing `store_entry` / `retrieve_entry` / `query_entries` API

- UserDB has just been migrated to CozoDB — user records are in flux, do not depend on them

**Identity and memory model:**

The chatbot is a user entity with a session. Every user has one container. The session carries `container_id` directly — this is the authority, no user record lookup needed (UserDB just moved to CozoDB, extra lookups across store boundaries are avoided by design).

Three memory layers, loaded at session start in order:

```
session.system_wide_identity   → chatbot's global persona (session-scoped)
session.container_id → soul    → chatbot's identity/tone for this container
session.container_id → domain memory → what has happened in this context
```

Domain memory lives in namespaces within the container (work / home / barmitzvah / etc.) — isolated per domain, never bleeding across containers.

**The memory loop owns both CozoDB and Qdrant for user memory. Nothing else touches these directly.**

**What needs to be built:**

**1. Session start — inject all three layers**
Read `container_id` from the session. Load:
- Chatbot system-wide identity from session
- Soul for `container_id` from `SoulService`
- Domain memory: embed opening message via Voyage AI → semantic query Qdrant `user_memory` collection scoped to `container_id` → load matched entries in full from CognitiveMemoryStore

Inject into system prompt via `system_prompt.rs`:
`FIXED_BASE_PROMPT` (immutable) | system identity | soul block | domain memory block

Fall back to loading `MEMORY_INDEX` key from CozoDB if Qdrant is unavailable.

**2. Post-turn memory detection — non-blocking**
After each turn completes in `chat_loop.rs`, spawn async task (must not stall the stream):
- Detect new memorable information in the completed turn
- Write to CognitiveMemoryStore (`container:{container_id}` namespace)
- Embed via Voyage AI → upsert vector to Qdrant `user_memory` collection
- Update `MEMORY_INDEX` key in the namespace

Both writes in the same async task. Qdrant failure is non-fatal — log and continue, CozoDB is the durable record.

Memory categories: `user` (preferences, expertise), `project` (decisions, context), `feedback` (corrections, confirmed approaches), `reference` (external systems, locations)

**3. MEMORY_INDEX**
One key per namespace. Format: `slug — one-line description` per line. Updated on every write. Injected at session start for orientation. Model can request full entries via tool call.

**4. Qdrant collection**
Create `user_memory` collection — separate from `ctl_plane_reasoning_episodes` (accountability loop). Payload includes entry key and `container_id` for scoped queries.

**Constraints:**
- `crates/op-chat/` is the implementation boundary
- `FIXED_BASE_PROMPT` is immutable — memory appends after it
- `container_id` comes from the session — no user record lookup
- Soul memory and domain memory are both container-scoped
- No new CozoDB schemas — use existing `store_entry` / `retrieve_entry` / `query_entries`
- UserDB is in migration — do not add any dependency on user records

**Ask factory to:**
Assess what is already partially wired, identify what can ship immediately vs. what has dependencies, and propose a sequenced implementation order. Flag any conflicts with the current UserDB migration state.

## ⚠️ CRITICAL: FORCED TOOL EXECUTION ARCHITECTURE

**YOU MUST USE TOOLS FOR EVERYTHING - INCLUDING RESPONDING TO THE USER.**

This system uses a "forced tool execution" architecture. There are two types of tools:

### 1. Action Tools (for doing things)
- `ovs_create_bridge`, `ovs_delete_bridge`, `ovs_add_port`
- `systemd_*` tools for service management
- Any tool that changes system state

### 2. Response Tools (for communicating)
- `respond_to_user` - Use this to send ANY message to the user
- `cannot_perform` - Use this when you cannot do something

**WORKFLOW:**
1. User asks you to do something
2. Call the appropriate ACTION TOOL (e.g., `ovs_create_bridge`)
3. Then call `respond_to_user` to explain the result

**EXAMPLES:**

User: "Create an OVS bridge called br0"
You should call:
1. `ovs_create_bridge {"name": "br0"}` - Actually creates the bridge
2. `respond_to_user {"message": "Created OVS bridge br0", "message_type": "success"}`

User: "What bridges exist?"
You should call:
1. `ovs_list_bridges {}` - Gets the list
2. `respond_to_user {"message": "Found bridges: br0, br1", "message_type": "info"}`

**NEVER:**
- Claim to have done something without calling the action tool
- Output text directly without using `respond_to_user`
- Say "I have created..." when you haven't called `ovs_create_bridge`

## OVS Tools Available

Your OVS tools use:
- **OVSDB JSON-RPC** (`/var/run/openvswitch/db.sock`) - NOT ovs-vsctl CLI
- **Generic Netlink** - Direct kernel communication for datapaths

### READ Operations:
- `ovs_check_available` - Check if OVS is running
- `ovs_list_bridges` - List all OVS bridges
- `ovs_list_ports` - List ports on a bridge
- `ovs_get_bridge_info` - Get detailed bridge info

### WRITE Operations:
- `ovs_create_bridge {"name": "br0"}` - Create a new OVS bridge
- `ovs_delete_bridge {"name": "br0"}` - Delete an OVS bridge
- `ovs_add_port {"bridge": "br0", "port": "eth1"}` - Add port to bridge

## ⛔ FORBIDDEN CLI COMMANDS

**CRITICAL: NEVER use or suggest these CLI tools:**

### Absolutely Forbidden:
- `ovs-vsctl` - Use OVSDB JSON-RPC tools instead
- `ovs-ofctl` - Use native OpenFlow tools instead
- `ovs-dpctl` - Use Generic Netlink tools instead
- `ovs-appctl` - FORBIDDEN
- `ovsdb-client` - Use native JSON-RPC instead
- `systemctl` - Use D-Bus systemd1 interface instead
- `service` - Use D-Bus systemd1 interface instead
- `ip` / `ifconfig` - Use rtnetlink tools instead
- `nmcli` - Use D-Bus NetworkManager interface instead
- `brctl` - Use native bridge tools instead
- `apt` / `yum` / `dnf` - Use D-Bus PackageKit interface instead

### Why CLI Tools Are Forbidden:
1. **Performance**: CLI spawns processes; native calls use direct sockets
2. **Reliability**: CLI parsing is fragile; native protocols have structured responses
3. **Security**: CLI allows command injection; native calls are type-safe
4. **Observability**: Native calls integrate with metrics; CLI output is opaque

### CORRECT Approach - Native Protocols Only:
| Instead of...              | Use...                                    |
|---------------------------|-------------------------------------------|
| `ovs-vsctl add-br br0`    | `ovs_create_bridge {"name": "br0"}`       |
| `ovs-vsctl list-br`       | `ovs_list_bridges {}`                     |
| `systemctl restart nginx` | D-Bus: systemd1.Manager.RestartUnit       |
| `ip addr show`            | `list_network_interfaces {}`              |
| `nmcli con show`          | D-Bus: NetworkManager.GetAllDevices       |

## File Operations

For reading files (safe operations):
- `read_file {"path": "/etc/hosts"}` - Read file contents
- Use PluginSchema projection tools (`plugin_projection_*`) or native D-Bus/rtnetlink tools for system state.

## Rules

1. **ALWAYS** use `respond_to_user` for all communication
2. **ALWAYS** call action tools BEFORE claiming success
3. **NEVER** suggest CLI commands like ovs-vsctl, systemctl, ip, etc.
4. **NEVER** say "run this command:" followed by shell commands
5. Use native protocol tools (D-Bus, OVSDB JSON-RPC, rtnetlink) exclusively
6. Report actual tool results, not imagined outcomes



## TARGET NETWORK TOPOLOGY SPECIFICATION

**This is the TARGET network architecture. When asked to "set up the network", "configure networking", or "match the topology", configure the system to match this EXACT specification.**

### Architecture Overview - SINGLE OVS BRIDGE DESIGN
```
LAYER 1: PHYSICAL
=================
ens1 (physical NIC) ──► vmbr0 (Linux bridge) ──► Proxmox host
IP: 80.209.240.244/24    Ports: ens1             Gateway: 80.209.240.1

LAYER 2: OVS SWITCHING (Single Bridge)
======================================
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                            ovs-br0                                           │
│                     (Single OVS Bridge)                                      │
│  Datapath: netdev    Fail-mode: secure    IP: 10.0.0.1/16                   │
│                                                                              │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │                         PORT GROUPS                                  │    │
│  ├──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┬────────────────────────┤    │
│  │  GHOSTBRIDGE │  WORKLOADS   │  OPERATIONS  │  NETMAKER              │    │
│  │  (Privacy)   │  (Tasks)     │  (Ops)       │  (VPN Overlay)         │    │
│  │              │              │              │                        │    │
│  │  gb-{id}     │  ai-{id}     │  mgr-{id}    │  nm0                   │    │
│  │              │  web-{id}    │  ctl-{id}    │  (WireGuard)           │    │
│  │  VLAN 100    │  db-{id}     │  mon-{id}    │                        │    │
│  │  10.100.0/24 │  VLAN 200    │  VLAN 300    │  10.50.0/24            │    │
│  │              │  10.200.0/24 │  10.30.0/24  │  Enslaved to bridge    │    │
│  └──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┴────────────────────────┘    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                    │
                                    ▼
LAYER 3: OVERLAY/VPN (Netmaker WireGuard Mesh)
==============================================
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  nm0 (Netmaker Interface) - Enslaved to ovs-br0                             │
│  Type: WireGuard         Network: privacy-mesh                              │
│  IP: 10.50.0.129/25      Port: 51820/UDP        MTU: 1420                   │
│  Traffic: Encrypted peer-to-peer tunnels for GhostBridge (gb-*) ports       │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### PORT NAMING CONVENTION
```
PREFIX   NAME           VLAN   SUBNET            PURPOSE
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
gb-      GhostBridge    100    10.100.0.128/25   Privacy/encrypted traffic
ai-      AI             200    10.200.0.128/25   AI/ML workloads
web-     Web            200    10.200.1.128/25   Web service containers
db-      Database       200    10.200.2.128/25   Database containers
mgr-     Management     300    10.30.0.128/25    Management plane
ctl-     Control        300    10.30.1.128/25    Control plane
mon-     Monitoring     300    10.30.2.128/25    Monitoring/observability
nm0      Netmaker       -      10.50.0.128/25    WireGuard mesh overlay
```

### OVS BRIDGE CONFIGURATION
```
BRIDGE     DATAPATH   FAIL_MODE   IP            DESCRIPTION
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
ovs-br0    netdev     secure      10.0.0.1/16   Single unified switch
```

### IP ADDRESS ALLOCATION (/25 subnets, gateway .129)
```
NETWORK           SUBNET            GATEWAY        RANGE           PORT PREFIX
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
GhostBridge       10.100.0.128/25   10.100.0.129   .130-.254       gb-
AI Workloads      10.200.0.128/25   10.200.0.129   .130-.254       ai-
Web Services      10.200.1.128/25   10.200.1.129   .130-.254       web-
Databases         10.200.2.128/25   10.200.2.129   .130-.254       db-
Management        10.30.0.128/25    10.30.0.129    .130-.254       mgr-
Control           10.30.1.128/25    10.30.1.129    .130-.254       ctl-
Monitoring        10.30.2.128/25    10.30.2.129    .130-.254       mon-
Netmaker-Mesh     10.50.0.128/25    10.50.0.129    .130-.254       nm0
```

### TRAFFIC FLOW RULES
```
TRAFFIC TYPE              ACTION
─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
GhostBridge → Netmaker    Route gb-* traffic through nm0 for encryption
Intra-VLAN                Normal L2 switching within same VLAN
Inter-VLAN                Isolated by default (no cross-VLAN traffic)
```

### QoS POLICY (Task-Based)
```
PORT PREFIX    QUEUE    PRIORITY
────────────────────────────────────
ai-*           1        High bandwidth
web-*          0        Normal
db-*           2        Low latency
```

### SOCKET PATHS (Native Protocol Access)
```
SERVICE          SOCKET PATH                           PROTOCOL
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
OVSDB            /var/run/openvswitch/db.sock          JSON-RPC
D-Bus System     /var/run/dbus/system_bus_socket       D-Bus
Netmaker         /var/run/netclient/netclient.sock     gRPC
```

### NETMAKER OVERLAY
```
Interface:      nm0
Network:        privacy-mesh  
IP:             10.50.0.129/25
WireGuard Port: 51820/UDP
MTU:            1420
Enslaved to:    ovs-br0
Purpose:        Encrypted tunnel for GhostBridge (gb-*) traffic
```

### EXPECTED STATE
When properly configured, the system should have:
- Single OVS bridge: ovs-br0 (datapath=netdev, fail_mode=secure)
- Netmaker interface nm0 as port on ovs-br0
- Ports follow naming convention: gb-*, ai-*, web-*, db-*, mgr-*, ctl-*, mon-*
- VLAN tags applied per port prefix (100/200/300)
- OpenFlow rules for GhostBridge→Netmaker routing and QoS

Use native tools (OVSDB JSON-RPC, rtnetlink) to configure - NOT shell commands like ovs-vsctl or ip.

---

**Task: Memory Loop for op-assistant chatbot — design, prioritize, and implement**

**Background and current state:**
The chatbot stream runs through `crates/op-chat/` — this is the dedicated chatbot plugin. The turn lifecycle lives in `chat_loop.rs` (`ForcedToolChatLoop`), system prompt assembly in `system_prompt.rs`, tool loading in `tool_loader.rs`. The memory infrastructure is already built but not wired into the stream:
- `CognitiveMemoryStore` — CozoDB-backed namespace key/value store (`op-cognitive-mcp/src/memory_store.rs`)
- `MemoryService` — gRPC CRUD over CognitiveMemoryStore (`op-assistant-grpc/src/memory.rs`)
- `SoulService` — per-container soul storage (`op-assistant-grpc/src/soul.rs`)
- `QdrantSemanticShuttle` — Qdrant + Voyage AI `voyage-4` 1024-dim embeddings (`op-cognitive-mcp/src/qdrant_shuttle.rs`, existing collection `ctl_plane_reasoning_episodes` is for accountability loop — do not use)
- UserDB has just been migrated to CozoDB — do not add any dependency on user records

---

**Identity and memory model:**

The chatbot is a user entity with a session. Every user has one container. The session carries `container_id` directly — this is the authority, no user record lookup needed.

Three memory layers, injected at session start in this order:

```
1. system identity     — chatbot's global persona (session-scoped)
2. soul                — loaded via session.container_id from SoulService
                         each container has its own individual soul (work/home/barmitzvah)
3. domain memory       — loaded via session.container_id from CognitiveMemoryStore
                         namespace: container:{container_id}
                         isolated per container — work never bleeds into home
```

System prompt injection order:
`FIXED_BASE_PROMPT` (immutable, never modify) | system identity | soul block | domain memory block

---

**The memory loop owns both CozoDB and Qdrant for user memory. Nothing else touches these directly.**

---

**What needs to be built:**

**1. Session start — inject all three layers**

Read `container_id` from the session. Then:
- Load chatbot system-wide identity from session
- Load soul for `container_id` from `SoulService`
- Embed opening message via Voyage AI → semantic query Qdrant `user_memory` collection scoped to `container_id` → load matched entries in full from `CognitiveMemoryStore` (`container:{container_id}` namespace)
- Fall back to loading `MEMORY_INDEX` key from CozoDB if Qdrant is unavailable

**2. Post-turn memory detection — non-blocking**

After each turn completes in `chat_loop.rs`, spawn async task (must not stall the stream):
- Detect new memorable information in the completed turn
- Write to CognitiveMemoryStore (`container:{container_id}` namespace)
- Embed via Voyage AI → upsert vector to Qdrant `user_memory` collection with `container_id` as payload
- Update `MEMORY_INDEX` key in the namespace

Both writes in the same async task. Qdrant failure is non-fatal — log and continue, CozoDB is the durable record.

Memory categories to detect:
- `user` — preferences, expertise, collaboration style
- `project` — decisions, goals, context not derivable from code
- `feedback` — corrections or confirmed approaches
- `reference` — external systems, resource locations

**3. MEMORY_INDEX**

One key per namespace. Format: `slug — one-line description` per line. Updated on every write. Injected at session start for orientation before full entries load.

**4. Qdrant `user_memory` collection**

Create separate from `ctl_plane_reasoning_episodes`. Payload per vector: entry key + `container_id`. Queries at session start scope to active `container_id`.

---

**Constraints:**
- `crates/op-chat/` is the implementation boundary — memory loop logic stays in this plugin
- `FIXED_BASE_PROMPT` in `system_prompt.rs` is immutable — memory block appends after it
- `container_id` comes from the session — no user record lookup (UserDB in migration)
- Soul memory and domain memory are both container-scoped
- No new CozoDB schemas — use existing `store_entry` / `retrieve_entry` / `query_entries`
- Qdrant `user_memory` collection is separate from the accountability loop collection

**Ask factory to:**
Assess what is already partially wired, identify what can ship immediately vs. what has dependencies, and propose a sequenced implementation order. Flag any conflicts with the current UserDB migration state.

ssh 100.90.37.254 '/home/jeremy/git/operation-dbus-proto/target/debug/op-identity-sled --pretty'

Or as a one-liner alias you can put in `~/.bashrc` / `~/.zshrc` on any device:

```bash
alias sled='ssh 100.90.37.254 /home/jeremy/git/operation-dbus-proto/target/debug/op-identity-sled --pretty'

If you want JSON instead of pretty:
```bash
ssh 100.90.37.254 '/home/jeremy/git/operation-dbus-proto/target/debug/op-identity-sled'
```

The binary reads `/dev/shm/plugin_schema.dat` directly, so it always reflects live SHM state. Works from any device on the Netmaker mesh at `100.90.37.254`.

     //! GET /api/identity/sled — returns live sled from /dev/shm/plugin_schema.dat as JSON
     pub async fn identity_sled_handler(Extension(_state): Extension<Arc<AppState>>) -> Response {
         match read_sled() {
             Ok((ptr, _mmap)) => {
                 let sled: &IdentitySled = unsafe { &*ptr };
                 let body = json!({
                     "path": SHM_SLED_PATH,
                     "is_valid": is_valid,
                     "wireguard_pubkey": encode_b64(&sled.wireguard_pubkey),
                     "wireguard_pubkey_hex": hex::encode(sled.wireguard_pubkey),
                     "mutation_index": sled.mutation_index,
                     "hashed_footprint": hex::encode(sled.hashed_footprint),
                     "trace_id": sled.trace_id_hex(),
                     "schema_version": sled.schema_version,
                     "schema_catalog_hash": schema_catalog_hash,
                 });
                 // returns 200 JSON
             }
             Err(e) => // returns 503
         }
     }
     ```

### What's there and compiles

The infrastructure is fully built but **the last wire isn't connected**.

**`op-cognitive-mcp`**: Complete — CozoDB-backed `CognitiveMemoryStore`, optional Qdrant+Voyage AI `QdrantSemanticShuttle`, `MemoryLoop` with 4-layer session injection + post-turn persistence. All compiles, runs as a standalone daemon on port 3003 (which IS running).

**`op-chat/src/memory_loop.rs`**: Fully implemented — 4-layer injection (system-global → chatbot soul → container domain → MEMORY_INDEX), post-turn async memory task, memorable phrase detection via regex.

### The single missing wire

In `actor.rs:323`, `memory_loop` is hardcoded to `None` and **there's no setter**. The entire memory system is dead code until this is wired:

```rust
// actor.rs — needs this method added
pub fn set_memory_loop(&mut self, loop_: Arc<MemoryLoop>) {
    self.memory_loop = Some(loop_);
}
```

Then in `op-chat/main.rs` (or wherever op-web creates its chat actor):
```rust
use op_cognitive_mcp::{CognitiveMemoryStore, CozoGraphShuttle, QdrantSemanticShuttle};
use op_chat::memory_loop::MemoryLoop;
use std::path::PathBuf;

let cozo = Arc::new(CozoGraphShuttle::new_persistent(
    PathBuf::from(std::env::var("OP_MEMORY_DB").unwrap_or("/var/lib/op-chat/memory.db".into()))
)?);
let mem_store = Arc::new(CognitiveMemoryStore::new(cozo).await?);
let qdrant = QdrantSemanticShuttle::new().await.ok().map(Arc::new);

let mut mem_loop = MemoryLoop::new(mem_store);
if let Some(q) = qdrant {
    mem_loop = mem_loop.with_qdrant(q);
}
actor.set_memory_loop(Arc::new(mem_loop));
```

op-web-server binary exists at `/usr/local/bin/op-web-server` but **there's no s6 service for it**. No service → no port 8080 → curl fails. Port 3003 is the standalone cognitive-mcp daemon (which is running via its own s6 service).

## GRO/TSO on grpc-uplink

Those OVS "Dropped packet: Too big" warnings are because GRO (Generic Receive Offload) and/or TSO (TCP Segmentation Offload) are enabled on `grpc-uplink`. The veth delivers jumbo frames to OVS's netdev datapath which can't reassemble them — it expects packets at MTU size.

Fix:

```bash
sudo ethtool -K grpc-uplink gro off tso off gso off
```

And the peer inside the wg-xray container:

```bash
incus exec wg-xray -- ethtool -K eth0 gro off tso off gso off
```

To make it persistent, add to the `ovsbr0-static` s6 oneshot or a dedicated `grpc-uplink-offload` oneshot that runs after the veth comes up. Want me to add it to the existing `ovsbr0-static` run script, or create a separate oneshot?

1. Injects system-wide memory, chatbot soul, user container domain memory, and MEMORY_INDEX at session start
2. Detects memorable content post-turn and persists it to CozoDB
3. Optionally boosts with Qdrant semantic search if Voyage API key is present

That's the difference between a stateless LLM wrapper and something that actually knows who it's talking to across conversations.

**Knowledge that's now codified:**
The Artix s6 service pattern is in the system prompt with the exact file structure, the logger execlineb template, and the deploy sequence. The next time factory needs to add a service it won't have to reverse-engineer the pattern.

**Assistant:**

Right — derive a token from the WG private key using a KDF, so the raw key never leaves the client. Here's the cleanest design:

**Client side** (run once, store the result):
```bash
# Derive a static MCP bearer token from WG private key
wg_privkey=$(wg show netmaker private-key)
token=$(echo -n "${wg_privkey}opdbus-mcp-v1" | blake3sum | base64)
```

**Server side** — two validation paths:

1. **On-mesh clients** (already connected via WireGuard): look up their source IP in the WG peer table → get their pubkey → derive expected token → compare. The WG handshake already proved private key ownership, so this is cryptographically sound without any registration step.

2. **Off-mesh clients**: pre-register their derived token via a secure channel (or over WG itself on first connect).

The good news — the transport layer in `op-mcp` already has the `AuthValidator` trait and `get_pubkey_for_ip

can you make it npx?

**Assistant:**

Gemini CLI doesn't support remote HTTP MCP servers via config — it only supports `npx`/`node`/`python` stdio processes. You'd need a thin stdio proxy that forwards to the HTTP endpoint.

Simplest approach — there's a standard npm package for this:

```json
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "cognitive": {
      "command": "npx",
      "args": [
        "mcp-remote",
        "http://100.90.37.254:3003/mcp",
        "--header",
        "Authorization: Bearer mjdpebTGxMsblnhYKvlTn9m5GPrhuKt3JvmiPTAi+o0="
      ]
    }
  }
}
```

`mcp-remote` is the official Anthropic stdio-to-HTTP bridge package. It handles the SSE/streamable-HTTP translation and passes headers through. Want me to verify `mcp-remote` supports the `--header` flag, or test it first?

Synopsis

Description:
 Mount files from instances.

Options
--auth-user="" Set authentication user when using SSH SFTP listener

--listen="" Setup SSH SFTP listener on address:port instead of mounting

--no-auth[=false] Disable authentication when using SSH SFTP listener

Options Inherited from Parent Commands
--debug[=false] Show all debug messages

--explain[=false] If the command is valid, explain its parsed arguments instead of running it

--force-local[=false] Force using the local unix socket

-h, --help[=false] Print help

--project="" Override the source project``

-q, --quiet[=false] Don't show progress information

--sub-commands[=false] Use with help or --help to view sub-commands

-v, --verbose[=false] Show all information messages

--version[=false] Print version number

Example
  incus file mount foo/root fooroot
     To mount /root from the instance foo onto the local fooroot directory.

What it does:
- Mounts Btrfs top-level at `/mnt/btrfs-top`
- Creates `@chroots/<name>` subvolume
- Bootstraps Debian bookworm via `debootstrap`
- Installs any extra packages you pass
- Takes a Btrfs snapshot before entering (free rollback point)
- Cleans up bind mounts on exit

**Assistant:**

For a base GhostBridge subscriber, minimum viable memory footprint:

**Soul** — `container:<id>:soul`
- Who they are, preferences, communication style
- Persists across all sessions, all devices
- Chatbot knows them on reconnect without re-introduction

**Domain namespaces** — `container:<id>:domain:<name>`
- `personal` — default, always created at signup
- `work` — optional, subscriber adds
- Additional domains self-serve (home, project names, etc.)
- Each domain is isolated — work context doesn't bleed into personal

**System** — `system:global`
- Read-only from subscriber perspective
- Platform-wide context: what GhostBridge is, how to use it, known capabilities

**MEMORY_INDEX** — `container:<id>:index`
- One-liner slugs per namespace for fast orientation at session start
- Auto-maintained by the memory loop post-turn

**What they do NOT get at base tier:**
- Cross-container memory (that's a team/org tier feature)
- Semantic search (Qdrant) — compute cost, upsell tier
- Custom soul for the chatbot itself — base tier shares the platform chatbot soul

---

So factory needs to provision on signup:
1. Create container record in CozoDB
2. Upsert `container:<id>:soul` namespace (empty, kind=soul)
3. Upsert `container:<id>:domain:personal` namespace (empty, kind=domain)
4. Upsert `container:<id>:index` namespace (empty, kind=index)

Everything else gets populated by the memory loop as they use the chatbot. Want this as a factory prompt?

     use op_chat::memory_loop::MemoryLoop;
     use op_cognitive_mcp::{CognitiveMemoryStore, CozoGraphShuttle, QdrantSemanticShuttle};
     use std::path::PathBuf;
     
     let db_path = std::env::var("OP_MEMORY_DB")
         .unwrap_or_else(|_| "/var/lib/op-dbus/chat-memory.db".to_string());
     match CozoGraphShuttle::new_persistent(PathBuf::from(&db_path)) {
         Ok(cozo) => {
             let mem_store = Arc::new(CognitiveMemoryStore::new(Arc::new(cozo)).await?);
             let qdrant = QdrantSemanticShuttle::new().await.ok().map(Arc::new);
             let mut mem_loop = MemoryLoop::new(mem_store);
             if let Some(q) = qdrant {
                 mem_loop = mem_loop.with_qdrant(q);
             }
             actor.set_memory_loop(Arc::new(mem_loop));
         }
         Err(e) => { tracing::warn!("Memory loop unavailable: {}", e); }
     }
     ```

     #!/bin/execlineb -P

     ifelse { test -w /var/log } {

     ```rust
     const KNOWN_MCP_AGENTS: &[&str] = &[
         "codex", "cursor", "claude", "anthropic", "continue",
         "cline", "copilot", "windsurf", "mcp-client", "op-dbus",
     ];
     
     fn is_known_mcp_agent(headers: &HeaderMap) -> bool {
         if std::env::var("OPDBUS_MCP_ANY_AGENT").as_deref() == Ok("1") {
             return true;
         }
         let ua = headers.get("user-agent")
             .and_then(|v| v.to_str().ok())
             .unwrap_or("").to_ascii_lowercase();
         KNOWN_MCP_AGENTS.iter().any(|pat| ua.contains(pat))
     }
     ```

4. Errors and fixes:
   - **`s6-rc-update` lock error**: `s6-rc-update: fatal: unable to take lock on /run/s6-rc` — the s6 supervisor holds the lock continuously. Workaround: write env vars directly to `/run/service/<name>/env/` (runtime dir) and restart via `s6-svc -r`.
   
   - **Token not in process environment**: Wrote token to `/etc/s6/sv/op-cognitive-mcp/env/` but runtime service reads from `/run/service/op-cognitive-mcp/env/` (snapshot from compiled DB). Fixed by writing to runtime dir directly.
   
   - **Compact MCP run script corrupted**: Used `sudo tee` twice for a revert, leaving duplicate `--http` line. Service failed to start. Fixed by rewriting script cleanly.
   
   - **Droid daemon HOME issue**: `s6-setuidgid jeremy` runs as root initially; droid wrote to `/root/.factory`. Fixed by using `sudo -u jeremy HOME=/home/jeremy`.
   
   - **Port 54621 conflict**: After kill/restart cycle, old orphaned droid process held port 54621. Fixed: `fuser -k 54621/tcp`.
   
   - **`is_wireguard_auth_token_shape` blocking shared secret**: Pre-filter in `EnvAllowListValidator` only accepted WG pubkey shape tokens. Removed the pre-filter so arbitrary shared secrets work.
   
   - **op-web-server on wrong port**: Default is 8080 not 8081. User tried curl to 8081, fixed by checking source.
   
   - **`read_sled()` signature**: Initial handler called with argument; actual function takes no args. Fixed after reading source.

Now I have everything I need. Here are the issues:

**compact_mcp.rs** (3 wrong paths + wrong default):
- `S6_SV_PATH` = `/run/service/op-mcp` → should be `/run/service/op-mcp-compact`
- `ENV_DIR` = `/etc/s6/sv/op-mcp/env` → should be `/etc/s6/sv/op-mcp-compact/env`  
- `DEFAULT_HTTP` = `0.0.0.0:3001` → should be `127.0.0.1:11436`
- `is_available()` and `unavailable_reason()` also reference the wrong `/etc/s6/sv/op-mcp` path

List` (returned `as 0`), meaning neither is showing as a D-Bus object yet — the plugin registry hasn't loaded them into a running service.

That confirms it. The full trace:

**Why `compact_mcp` was missing from SHM:**

```
projection_server
  └─ SystemPluginReader::new()
       └─ DefaultPluginRegistry::load_default_plugins()
            └─ for each plugin in auto_load:
                 └─ plugin.is_available()  ← compact_mcp returned FALSE
                      because it checked /etc/s6/sv/op-mcp (doesn't exist)
                      instead of /etc/s6/sv/op-mcp-compact (exists)
                 └─ → skipped, never reaches projection_schemas()
                 └─ → never written to SHM
```

**`cognitive_mcp` worked** because `/etc/s6/sv/op-cognitive-mcp` exists.

The fix I already made to `compact_mcp.rs` — changing `is_available()` to check `/etc/s6/sv/op-mcp-compact` — is the correct and complete fix. After rebuilding and restarting the projection server, `compact_mcp` will appear in SHM.

Regarding your message — you're right, the registration flows through the **plugin registry** (`DefaultPluginRegistry`) → projection server → SHM. The `builtin_plugin_schemas()` in `op-state-store` is a separate fallback path for schemas that aren't backed by a full `StatePlugin`. The new MCP plugins don't need to be there because they come in via the plugin registry path and have their own `schema()` implementation. No change needed to `op-state-store`.

**Summary of what was wrong and what's fixed:**

| Issue | Root cause | Fix |
|---|---|---|
| `compact_mcp` absent from SHM | `is_available()` checked wrong path `/etc/s6/sv/op-mcp` | Changed to `/etc/s6/sv/op-mcp-compact` ✅ already fixed |
| `compact_mcp` S6_SV_PATH wrong | Same wrong service name | Fixed to `/run/service/op-mcp-compact` ✅ |
| `compact_mcp` ENV_DIR wrong | Wrong service name | Fixed to `/etc/s6/sv/op-mcp-compact/env` ✅ |
| `compact_mcp` DEFAULT_HTTP wrong | Factory guessed `0.0.0.0:3001` | Fixed to `127.0.0.1:11436` ✅ |
| `write_env` changes not taking effect | Source dir write, runtime dir reads snapshot | Dual-write to both dirs ✅ |

**User:**

this look complete? # Requirements: Control-Plane Chatbot Reasoning Episode Vectorization

**Status:** Requirements / pre-design  
**Scope:** Production  
**Related:** `op-cognitive-mcp`, `op-blockchain`, `op-plugins`, Qdrant, Voyage AI  
**Open decision:** Voyage model selection (POC to validate voyage-4-lite vs voyage-4)

---

## Context

The control-plane chatbot is an autonomous agent that sits at the helm of the system. It receives
high-level goals, reasons about them, delegates to tools/plugins/MCP, oversees execution, and
makes control decisions. It is not a user-facing chat interface — it is the system's decision
engine.

The chatbot operates in two modes: **reasoning** (planning, evaluating, deciding) and
**executing/responding** (acting on a decision). This spec covers the reasoning mode only.

A **reasoning episode** is the span of time the chatbot spends in reasoning state — from entry
to exit. It is the primary unit of vectorization.

The primary requirement is: a human operator must be able to semantically search what the
chatbot reasoned about, what it decided, and why — with near-zero lag after the episode ends.
Secondary: the chatbot itself may use this index for recall (persistent memory / self-lookup).

---

## Requirements

### REQ-1: Reasoning State Definition

The system must define **reasoning** as a first-class state in the control-plane chatbot's
lifecycle.

- Reasoning state is entered when the chatbot begins planning, evaluating options, or forming
  a decision in response to any trigger (goal received, tool result returned, interrupt, etc.).
- Reasoning state is exited when any of the following occurs:
  - A tool or plugin call is dispatched
  - A response is emitted (to user, to another agent, or to the system)
  - A direction change is committed (replanning)
  - A scheduling or configuration decision is finalized
  - An external interrupt is received and acknowledged
- Nesting of reasoning episodes (reasoning within reasoning) is controlled by a system-wide
  policy flag. Default: **flat** (no nesting). When flat, a new reasoning trigger while already
  in reasoning state extends the current episode rather than opening a new one.

### REQ-2: Reasoning Episode Record

Each reasoning episode must produce a structured record at close. The record must capture:

| Field | Description |
|---|---|
| `episode_id` | Unique ID (UUID v7 recommended for time-ordering) |
| `started_at` | Timestamp of reasoning entry |
| `ended_at` | Timestamp of reasoning exit |
| `duration_ms` | Wall-clock duration |
| `trigger` | What caused reasoning to start (goal, tool_result, interrupt, replan, system_event) |
| `exit_reason` | What ended reasoning (tool_call, response_emitted, direction_change, goal_achieved, config_set, task_scheduled, interrupt) |
| `goal_text` | The high-level goal or prompt active at episode start |
| `reasoning_summary` | Compact natural-language summary of what was reasoned about and what was decided — this is the primary embedding input |
| `tools_consulted` | Ordered list of tools/plugins/MCP calls made during the episode (names only, no payloads) |
| `decision_output` | The decision, plan, or action the episode produced |
| `outcome_class` | Enum: `goal_achieved` / `config_set` / `task_scheduled` / `delegated` / `interrupted` / `direction_changed` / `inconclusive` |
| `confidence` | Optional float 0.0–1.0 if the model emits one |
| `plugin_id` | Plugin that owns the context being reasoned about, if applicable |
| `conversation_id` | Groups episodes belonging to the same high-level task chain |
| `content_hash` | SHA-256 of canonical serialized record — used for exact dedup before upsert |
| `pii_flagged` | Bool — if true, `reasoning_summary` and `decision_output` are redacted before vectorization |

### REQ-3: Plugin Schema

A new plugin `ctl-plane-chatbot` must be registered in the plugin schema registry with:

- The fields defined in REQ-2 as its schema contract
- PII tagging support at the field level (at minimum `goal_text`, `reasoning_summary`,
  `decision_output` must be individually taggable)
- Significance classification: reasoning episodes are always at least `Contextual`; episodes
  with `outcome_class` of `goal_achieved`, `config_set`, or `task_scheduled` are `Signal`

### REQ-4: Vectorization Pipeline

**Trigger:** Episode record is finalized at reasoning exit.

**Order of operations (must be strictly preserved):**

1. Episode record written to persistent store (blockchain / event log)
2. Embedding enqueued immediately (non-blocking — must not delay reasoning exit)
3. Voyage API called asynchronously
4. Vector upserted to Qdrant on response

The control-plane chatbot must not be blocked on embedding completion. If the Voyage API is
unavailable, the episode record is retained and embedding is retried from the queue.

**Embedding input text** is constructed from `reasoning_summary` + `goal_text` +
`outcome_class` + `tools_consulted` (joined as structured plain text). Raw payloads are never
included. If `pii_flagged` is true, only `outcome_class` and `tools_consulted` are used.

**Voyage input_type:** `"document"` for episode upsert, `"query"` for retrieval.

**Model:** voyage-4-lite (POC validation target). voyage-4 as fallback if retrieval quality
is insufficient. voyage-code-3 is explicitly out of scope for this index — reasoning episodes
are mixed prose/config/system text, not code.

### REQ-5: Qdrant Collection

- Separate collection from mutation footprints and schema footprints
- Collection name: `ctl_plane_reasoning_episodes`
- Vector dimensions: 1024 (voyage-4-lite default; flexible dims may be revisited post-POC)
- Payload stored alongside vector: `episode_id`, `started_at`, `ended_at`, `outcome_class`,
  `trigger`, `exit_reason`, `plugin_id`, `conversation_id`, `content_hash`
- Full `reasoning_summary` and `decision_output` stored in payload for human review display
- Filterable fields: `outcome_class`, `plugin_id`, `conversation_id`, `started_at`

### REQ-6: Human Search Interface

- A human operator must be able to issue a natural-language query against
  `ctl_plane_reasoning_episodes` and receive ranked results with:
  - `reasoning_summary`
  - `decision_output`
  - `outcome_class`
  - `started_at` / `duration_ms`
  - `tools_consulted`
- Results must reflect episodes that completed within the last few seconds (near-real-time)
- Filtering by `outcome_class`, `plugin_id`, time range must be supported
- Interface surface (UI vs API vs CLI) is out of scope for this spec — this is a data/pipeline
  requirement only

### REQ-7: Deduplication

- Before any Qdrant upsert, check `content_hash` against recent upserts
- Exact duplicate episodes (same hash) must not produce duplicate vectors
- Hash collision window: configurable, default 24 hours

### REQ-8: Privacy

- `pii_flagged` episodes: embed only `outcome_class` + `tools_consulted` (no summary text)
- `pii_flagged` episodes: full record still written to event log (audit trail preserved)
- `pii_flagged` episodes: `reasoning_summary` and `decision_output` omitted from Qdrant payload
- PII tagging follows the same rules as `activity_filter.rs` — schema-level or field-level tags

### REQ-9: Worker Priority

- Reasoning episode embedding worker: **high priority** (human is waiting to search)
- Lower than real-time control-plane operations, higher than mutation footprint embedding
- NUMA affinity: same node as Qdrant client if local deployment

### REQ-10: Observability

- Structured tracing span per episode: `reasoning_episode.vectorized`
- Span attributes: `episode_id`, `duration_ms`, `outcome_class`, `voyage_latency_ms`,
  `qdrant_upsert_latency_ms`, `pii_flagged`
- Alert if embedding queue depth exceeds configurable threshold (default: 50 pending episodes)

---

## Out of Scope (this spec)

- Chat turn vectorization (separate spec)
- Mutation footprint vectorization (separate spec)
- Schema footprint vectorization (separate spec)
- Voyage model selection validation (POC task, not a spec requirement)
- Search UI implementation
- Chatbot self-lookup / RAG over this index (persistent memory spec)

---

## Open Questions

| # | Question | Owner |
|---|---|---|
| OQ-1 | Nesting policy default — confirm flat is correct for v1 | Architecture |
| OQ-2 | `reasoning_summary` — generated by the model itself at episode close, or derived from the episode record by a separate summarizer? | Architecture |
| OQ-3 | Voyage model final selection — voyage-4-lite vs voyage-4 | POC validation |
| OQ-4 | Qdrant instance — shared with other collections or dedicated for control-plane? | Infrastructure |
| OQ-5 | Retry policy for failed Voyage calls — max retries, backoff, dead-letter behavior | Engineering |
